# V2 World Design

Working design surface for the world behind the MCP. Derived from a design
conversation, not a session log — the Ship's Computer entries cover what
happened, this covers what it should be.

**Status markers used throughout:**

- **Settled** — operator decided it.
- **Open** — named, undecided.
- **Unratified** — assistant proposal, not yet accepted or rejected.
- **Superseded** — was proposed, then killed. Full trail in §15.

Nothing in here is built. No code exists.

---

## 1. The three surfaces

Decision 1 from session 003 — *where the line sits between server state and
repo files* — was underspecified because it was framed as a division between
two things. There are three.

| Surface | Operator | Assistant |
|---|---|---|
| Repo | reads | reads |
| MCP world state | **blind** | authors |
| DoD of the research project | holds | reads (it's in the README) |

**Settled:** the third surface is the DoD, not a hidden win condition. The
DoD is not a peer of the other two — it's the plane both are judged against.
Repo and MCP are *state*; the DoD is *authority*.

The DoD, restated from the root README: mechanically take an idea down the
belt — into BoN tasks, operator picks the best interpretation, approved tasks
go to the Splitter which weighs them into a task, a full executable spec, or a
project decomposed into a batch of specs. Plus the clause that does the most
work and is easiest to skip: **applicable in a corporate setting instead of
one guy's PC.**

**Unratified — the repo/server line.** An artifact lives in the repo if the
pipeline's output depends on it being inspectable; it lives in server state if
inspecting it would let the operator route around the work.

- **Repo = the trace.** Traceable routing is both an improvement goal and a
  hard DoD requirement. A trace nobody can audit isn't a trace.
- **Server = the gate.** Conditions, weighing, checks. Hidden because a
  visible gate is a gameable gate.

...

```
**Unratified — the repo/server line.** An artifact lives in the repo if the
pipeline's output depends on it being inspectable; it lives in server state if
inspecting it would let the operator route around the work.

- **Repo = the trace.** Traceable routing is both an improvement goal and a
  hard DoD requirement. A trace nobody can audit isn't a trace.
- **Server = the gate.** Conditions, weighing, checks. Hidden because a
  visible gate is a gameable gate.

**Open — the auditability tension.** *"Operator never reads the cartridge"*
sits badly with the corporate clause. No buyer accepts a pipeline whose
decision logic they may not audit. Probable resolution: hiding is scoped to
**content** (the specific weights, gates, layout) while **mechanism** stays
fully auditable in the repo. If the cartridge hides *how* the Splitter
decides rather than *what* it decided, the thing being smoke-tested isn't
shippable.

---

## 2. What actually accumulates

**Settled.** The resource that survives resets is data and new knowledge. The
planet is disposable. If the world goes off course, reset and keep collecting.

**Settled, and the load-bearing claim:** taking a small amount of intent and
leveraging it into a directionally correct complex series of steps **is the
work**. World-building is not a preliminary to the pipeline — it is an
instance of it, running at the meta level, first and cheapest. Each
world-building failure is data that prevents the next one. Same shape as the
pipeline further down the belt.

**Consequence — the world doesn't need to be correct, it needs to be
instrumented.** An off-course world that fails silently yields nothing. An
off-course world that fails legibly is a complete run of the pipeline under
research.

**Consequence — priority inverts.** If the only thing surviving reset is data,
the extraction and refinement line is the project and the world is the test
fixture. Sessions 001–003 spent their design attention the other way round:
the world got the thinking, the pumpjack was built once and left at red tier.
Worth making that a decision rather than a drift.

**Unratified — the reflective toll.** The README says STOP STARTING OVER in
caps and the operator has granted unlimited permission to restart. Both hold
only if the boundary is mechanical. §5 settles that the *mechanical* cost of a
reset should fall over time; the proposal here is that the *reflective* cost
stays fixed — **a reset is legal only after a filed entry naming what the
world failed at.** Pay the toll, then burn it, which also makes the reset
produce the resource rather than spend it.

---

## 3. Gates, and how progress is measured

**Settled.** With enough new knowledge and enough repeats of the beginning,
any test designed to gate progress becomes trivial.

**Consequence — triviality is the readout, not the failure.** Knowledge is not
measured directly. It is measured by **how long a gate holds before it stops
being an obstacle.** Time-to-trivial is the instrument. Same way you'd rank
yourself in the original game: not "do I understand a main bus" but "how fast
to blue."

**Consequence — gates get retired and replaced at a higher abstraction, never
made harder.** The early game doesn't get harder; the map opens further out.
Adding resistance to a gate you've outgrown is fake difficulty. The ladder is
already written in the README: task → spec → decomposed project → *the level
above that the operator said he'd probably stop before*. That last one is the
next gate, reachable only once the one below goes trivial.

**Open — the leak/learned ambiguity.** A gate goes trivial for two reasons
that are indistinguishable from the inside: it was learned, or it leaked
(a route around was found, or the assistant pre-empted it and built something
shaped to pass). Decay rate is a valid metric **only** if gate integrity is
verified independently. Otherwise erosion reads as progress, and it will feel
exactly like getting better. This is the cheating detector flagged in the
Captain's Log, now load-bearing on the measurement rather than only on honesty.

**Resolved — what consumes science.** Not reading. Science is consumed **at
gate-design time**, spent to build the next constraint, which retires the
previous one. Research in, next tier of buildings out. This makes the
assistant the consumer, so retrieval must serve gate design rather than
browsing.

---

## 4. The Ship, and the knowledge base

**Settled — the Ship never resets.** The planet is disposable; the Ship is not.
This closes the one-server-or-two question by giving it a diegetic anchor: the
Ship is obviously the permanent process because it's the thing you arrived in.

**Settled — the Ship's navigation system is the knowledge base.** It carries a
set schema and existing entries, and each new run adds to it rather than
starting clean.

**Correction on why it's served over MCP.** Resets were never the threat — git
already survives resets, permanently and auditably, and a planet reset is a
repo operation that never touches `The Lab/`. The real reason is **retrieval**:
a flat directory of prose is not queryable at gate-design time.
`science.query(stage, tier)` is.

**Unratified — git is the store of record, MCP is the index.** Entries stay as
files in the repo: public, versioned, auditable, on the trace. The server
compiles and serves them. Server dies, rebuild the index; git dies, the project
is gone. Same crude/render split already made at the pumpjack, one layer up —
called the strongest design decision in the session 002 log. If the compiled
layer becomes authoritative rather than derived, building-in-public and the
corporate audit clause are both lost in one move.

### 4a. What the nav schema is for

A navigation log is only useful because something doesn't move between voyages.
That forces the question the fractions have been dodging: **what is invariant
across resets?** Not the map — new seed. Not the ore layout. What's invariant
is the *physics*: the pipeline, and how the operator behaves under it.

**Unratified — the schema's job is to force physics, not terrain, at write
time.**

- *"Iron was scarce in run 3"* — terrain. Worthless next world.
- *"When keys are cheap I stop generalising"* — physics. True in every world,
  and falsifiable against the next run.

That filter is what makes the fraction set non-arbitrary, and it gives a handle
on the leak/learned problem in §3, because a physics claim can be checked
against what the next run actually does.

**Unratified — derive the schema, don't design it.** A set schema at entry zero
is templating before the data, which is the operator's own doctrine and the
failure that has caught this project three times in the logs. But entry zero
has passed: four science entries and three Ship's Computer logs already exist.
The schema should be extracted from those.

**Open — does the nav schema itself evolve?** If the Ship never resets, a fixed
schema is designed at the moment of least knowledge and calcifies. An evolving
one needs migration for existing entries. This is the permanent-frontmatter-key
problem one level up, and §12 may answer it: the schema is subject to the same
selection pressure as everything else.

---

## 5. Persistence and reset

**Settled — the ontology and created frontmatter variables do not reset
either.** A reset is not a wipe. The hard-won schema carries; the planet does
not.

**Settled — this makes resets get easier over time**, dropping off in cost as
the ontology becomes established and the frontmatter choices accumulate.

**Consequence — reset cost is the readout of ontology quality.** Early resets
hurt because there's no ontology to carry. Late ones are cheap because the
durable part survives. Which means: **if reset 8 still hurts as much as reset
2, the ontology isn't doing any work.** Free to instrument, falsifiable, and it
measures accumulation rather than activity — the same instrument as
time-to-trivial in §3, pointed at a different axis.

**Consequence — the curtain becomes two-way.** Rarity (§11) is the assistant's
lever on operator behaviour. **The accumulated ontology is the operator's lever
on world building**, because whatever gets built has to be expressible in the
keys already established — or has to deliberately break them. This is the first
information flowing from the operator's side of the curtain to the assistant's,
and it happens without the operator ever reading the cartridge.

**The failure mode this creates, and its answer.** An ontology that always
survives a reset never has to defend itself, and becomes unfalsifiable — you
accumulate keys that fit every world precisely because none of them ever fought
one. The answer is §12: carry-forward with an adversary.

**Unratified — mechanical cost falls, reflective cost does not.** See §2. If
resets get cheap in both senses, "start over" is free again.

---

## 6. Primitives: ores as input templates

**Settled.** Primitives are input templates for different things, each
represented by a minable ore.

**Open — the pairs.** Tentative:

| Ore | Template for | Class |
|---|---|---|
| Coal | Guy actions — carry an item to a new place, explore for new ore, deconstruct/move a miner | **Fuel** — consumed, produces nothing, enables everything |
| Copper | Writing / designing new skills | Artifact |
| Stone | Creating new frontmatter variables | Artifact |
| Iron | Building executable docs | Artifact |
| Oil | Ontology entries | **Refined**, not assembled |
| Uranium | Altering the ontology template (see §7) | Late-game |

**Coal is a different class from copper/stone/iron.** Those three are pipeline
artifacts at ascending abstraction. Coal is fuel — true to the original, but it
means there are two resource *types*, not one taxonomy. Forcing them into one
schema will make the schema lie.

**Oil is the only pairing that is derived rather than assigned.** From the
session 002 call: ore is discrete and gets **assembled** (combined); oil is
continuous and gets **refined** (separated). An ontology entry takes one
undifferentiated notion and separates it into named fractions, and those
fractions are frontmatter keys. That pair passes the inserter test on its own.
Copper→skills does not yet.

**Unratified — settle scarcity before mapping.** Copper→skills / stone→keys /
iron→docs is currently swappable; nothing breaks if you rotate them, which is
the inserter test failing. What makes it non-arbitrary is mine time, already
noted in the Captain's Log. **The ore assignment is a statement about what you
want to be expensive.** Cheap stone means frontmatter keys get invented freely;
slow iron means every executable doc must earn itself. Decide the scarcity
curve first and the pairs fall out; decide pairs first and the justifications
get retrofitted.

**Unratified — recipes, not ores.** One ore → one output is a vending machine.
An executable doc costing iron *and* copper — because a doc no skill can
execute is inert — produces a dependency graph. That graph is traceable routing
arriving as game structure rather than as a goal to be studied.

---

## 7. The prose economy

**Settled — prose is capped absolutely and the cap never relaxes.**

- Executable tasks: ~10 words of prose.
- Skills: a few words of prose.
- Unlimited frontmatter variables, bought with stone.

**Settled — what scales is machinery, not permission.** Expressiveness does not
come from being allowed to say more. It comes from building more
info-management docs that convert variables into intent. Structure is free;
prose is rationed. The only way to say more is to invent a key.

**Settled — how complex skills get built.** An ontology entry (oil), combined
with a frontmatter schema *defined by* that ontology entry, is how a more
complex skill is assembled. Ontology entry = type definition. Frontmatter =
instance. Skill = the two combined.

**Note.** At ~10 words, an executable doc is almost entirely frontmatter, so
stone is buying *operational* keys, not descriptive ones. Those probably
shouldn't cost the same.

**Note.** A skill that is a name plus frontmatter, with behaviour derivable from
declared keys, is not *written* — it's **configured** from an ontology. That is
the operator's spec-section library, one abstraction level down, arriving from
the opposite direction. Reasonable evidence the model is right.

**Unratified — do not gate stone behind oil.** If a key requires a governing
ontology entry first, stone is inert until mid-game and ontology precedes data —
templating before the data again. Oil is mid-game in the original for a reason.
Let the early game accumulate ad-hoc keys and make a mess. **Oil is the
refactor**, and the refactor is where the ontology lesson actually lives. Nobody
designs an ontology first; they impose structure on a mess, and the imposing is
the skill.

**Open — is an ontology entry itself prose-capped?** This is the whole design or
the whole leak depending on the answer. If entries are exempt, they are the
escape hatch and everything fenced elsewhere drains through them — the essay
gets written there and called a definition. If capped, they must carry semantics
some other way, and the only visible route is **composition**: entries reference
other entries, meaning is built by combination rather than description.

**Open — uranium as a cap raise.** "Prose capped absolutely" and "uranium gives
more prose per entry" point opposite directions. A purchasable cap raise is how
constraint systems die — not through abuse, but because once 40 words is legal
you use 40, and the composition pressure quietly stops. Two proposed saves, both
unratified:

1. **Shape.** Uranium should buy *structure*, not word count. In the original,
   uranium is the only chain that behaves differently in kind — enrichment
   returns more input than it consumed. The analogue is an ontology entry that
   operates on other ontology entries. Meta-schema, self-refining. A genuine
   capability jump rather than a bigger budget.
2. **Timing.** The raise must arrive *after* the failure it solves. Hitting a
   definition that genuinely cannot be expressed by composition would be the
   most valuable single data point this system can produce — a real limit found
   at the frontier. Early uranium routes around that discovery before it happens.

---

## 8. Mining, sessions, and caps

**Settled.**

- Miners only mine while a session is cwd'd to their directory.
- The guy's skills are local to him, so a guy session must also be running.
- Therefore multiple parallel sessions are required to play. 6–8 concurrent is
  comfortable and **not** a constraint.
- Mine rate is wall-clocked.
- Mine output is capped at 5–10.
- Early primitives are basic and limited.

**This makes clobber prevention a requirement to play rather than an exercise.**
Improvement goal #8 arrives as the core loop.

**Note — the cap converts idling into a pulse rather than removing it.** Open,
let them cap, drain, repeat. That's hand-mining into a full inventory and it's
fine. But the number that matters is **cap ÷ cost**, not cap. If a drained chest
of 10 stone buys 10 frontmatter keys, the constraint never bites. Starvation is
the mechanic; the cap only produces it if consumption outruns a full chest.

**Note — caps on both ends is one coherent scarcity model.** Chest on input,
crate slots on output; congestion rather than decay, all the way through. Cost:
a fully backed-up system is a stall, and a stall is the most informative event
available, so it should be *visible* rather than something noticed by feeling
stuck.

**Open — do action templates occupy crate slots?** If not, cheap deconstruct
(§9) means the optimal play is constant churn — chase whatever the rarity curve
currently favours, move miners every session, pay nothing real. If they do
occupy slots, churn self-limits through congestion. They probably belong in the
same crates as everything else.

---

## 9. Expansion, deconstruction, and the real bottleneck

**Settled — the bottleneck is that making the resources for a new miner to
deploy on a patch is difficult.** This is the Factorio-true constraint: finding
ore has always been free, the drill is the cost.

**This dissolves the radar-abundance problem.** Discovery can be uncapped
because discovery is not capacity. A patch is inert until it can be afforded.

**Unratified — cost alone doesn't bind; competition does.** A miner must be
denominated in the same resources it competes with. Every stone spent on a miner
is a frontmatter key not created. If miners cost a separate expansion currency,
the tension evaporates and it becomes a tech tree rather than an economy. The
decision worth forcing is *invest in capacity or ship work*, which exists only
when both draw from one pool.

**Unratified — miners for radar-discovered types cost oil.** To mine a new kind
of thing you must first be able to say what kind of thing it is. Starting miners
are free (crash-landed with them), same as placing a burner drill by hand. Side
effect: you can *see* a patch you cannot yet define — the fog-of-war property
from session 003, arriving through the economy instead of through skill
visibility.

**Settled — deconstruct and move-miner are ordinary skills costing one action
template (coal), not build resources.** Nothing is lost, expansion decisions are
reversible, and early wrong calls don't compound. Correct for a system whose
premise is *make the mistake, capture the data*; expensive undo would fight the
loop.

**Note — the real cost of an action template is the declaration.** Paying coal
to move a miner means filling out a template: source, destination, what moved.
Every physical operation in the world therefore emits a filled artifact into
git. **Traceable routing stops being something to build and becomes the exhaust
of playing.** Improvement goal #2 as a byproduct.

---

## 10. Power

**Settled.**

- Power station and radar are buildings.
- Radar searches for new ore patches while the power station session is running.
- A unit of power has multiple consumers. An automated inserter costs 1; a radar
  costs ~10.
- Finding ore therefore means *not doing something else*.
```

- Finding ore therefore means *not doing something else*.

**Power prices automation, which turns doctrine into economics.** Manual →
template → automate stops being a discipline to maintain and becomes a budget
that can't be beaten. You cannot afford to automate early. Every automated
inserter is 1 power not looking for ore.

**Power is the first global state in this design.** Everything else is
per-directory — a patch, a chest, a guy. Power is one pool drawn on by 6–8
concurrent sessions, making it the first thing that forces the server to
arbitrate contention between them. Until now parallel sessions coexist; with a
shared bus they *interact*. Clobber prevention as a real concurrency problem
with a correct answer.

**Open — brownout behaviour.** Hard refusal teaches nothing, it just blocks.
**Proportional degradation** — everything slows at once — is the loud-failure
answer and the most instructive mechanic in the original, precisely because you
feel it before you can name it and then have to diagnose it. Unratified but
recommended.

**Coal is the master dial.** It pays for movement, exploration, deconstruct, and
plausibly power generation. Every physical operation routes through one
resource. True to the original, but it means coal scarcity is the global clock
speed of the world: loose and nothing has weight, tight and the world seizes. Of
all the curtain levers, the one to tune most carefully and log most strictly.

### 10a. Power and token spend

**Settled — power denominated in real spend forces optimisation.** Named
consequences: routing cheap actions to Haiku-class models, writing succinct
descriptions, pushing meaning into frontmatter rather than prose, and learning
to use cached inputs.

**Rule 4 check — clear, with one tripwire.** The README bans *"token spend
subbed for power and needing to spend more tokens to make more power so that I
can spend more tokens."* What's banned is the **loop** — spend generating the
resource that permits spend. What's proposed is **denomination** — a fixed
budget drawn against at true cost. The tripwire is generation: the moment power
is produced by uptime or by spend, it's the treadmill. **Keep the power station
as a meter, not a generator.**

**Caching is the deep one.** Cached input runs around a tenth the price of
fresh. The economically optimal shape is a large, stable, cacheable context —
ontology, lexicon, templates — with a tiny variable payload on top. That is
exactly the architecture being designed: heavy declared structure, ten words of
prose. **The constraint and the bill point the same direction**, which is rare;
gamified constraints usually fight the underlying economics. The cache is why
this design is right rather than merely elegant.

**Settled — cost is not the binding constraint.** 20x Max subscription; spend on
this project is negligible against normal work.

**Settled — rate-limit brownout is off by at least an order of magnitude** at
current scale. It does not arrive until 10+ specs are running at once.

**Consequence — two power sources, one handoff.** Early game needs an
*artificial* power budget, set below what's affordable, held behind the curtain.
Late game gets *real* grid pressure for free: concurrent sessions drawing on one
shared pool with a hard ceiling and a fixed refill rate is structurally a power
grid, and its brownout is exactly the wanted failure — everything degrades at
once and the greedy consumer must be diagnosed. **The handoff happens at the
DoD**: real pressure arrives precisely when running the batches of specs this
project is built to produce.

**Why the early budget must be behind the curtain.** The optimisation pressure
(Haiku routing, terseness, caching) only fires if the budget bites. A self-set
budget gets raised the first time it pinches during real work, and it won't feel
like cheating — it'll feel like being reasonable.

---

## 11. Radar and world generation

**Settled — rarity is a curtain lever, held by the assistant, not the
operator.** This removes the cheat surface entirely.

**It also makes the world responsive rather than correct.** Aiming at the DoD
happens continuously in reaction to operator behaviour, instead of being
specified up front: spam keys without generalising and stone gets rarer; haven't
hit the composition wall yet and uranium doesn't appear.

**Open — X% per tick makes the world unlearnable.** If radar rolls dice each
tick, ore distribution isn't a fact — it's generated on demand. Nothing can be
mapped, no theory about the world can be falsified, and beliefs get built out of
variance. Three quiet sessions of no iron reads identically as "iron is rare
here" and "unlucky."

**Unratified — seed, not dice.** Generate the layout once from a seed at world
creation; radar *reveals* what is already there. Exploration then means
something, theories can be wrong, and the reset story gets clean: new seed,
genuinely new world, ontology carried over intact per §5.

**Unratified — lever asymmetry.** An adaptive difficulty knob in the assistant's
hands is a machine for quietly removing friction, and CLAUDE.md already names the
assistant as the single largest threat to loud failure. Tightening is safe — it's
the product. Loosening is the dangerous direction. Proposal: scarcity may be
increased freely; **any loosening is a logged, dated, committed event.**

---

## 12. Adversarial selection

**Settled — each reset has a dialable adversarialness knob.** If the ontology
looks brittle, the world is designed to surface it, and the friction is
**destructive to the offending entry**.

**Settled — destructibility is the test, not a diagnosis.** Anything well built
shouldn't be brittle; anything brittle should break. There is no
brittleness-detection step. The world attacks; what survives was well built,
what shatters was not. The assistant holds *attack* authority, never *delete*
authority, and destruction is an outcome rather than a decision.

**Settled — it must not be a riddle.** When something breaks, the break has to
show its work: the specific input the key could not describe, the two entries
that collided, the case that had no home. A demonstration, not a verdict.
Otherwise the operator reverse-engineers deaths, which is exactly the quiet
failure this build exists to prevent.

**This answers deconstruct/migration, which came up three times.** Bad keys are
not refactored and not manually deleted. They are selected against. You never
decide to remove a key; the world proves it doesn't hold, and it burns.

**Unratified — destruction is a transfer, not a deletion.** When a key dies, the
record of what it was and why it failed lands in the Ship's nav log. Otherwise
the highest-value output of the system — evidence of a schema that didn't
survive contact — is destroyed along with the mistake. Ontology gets leaner, nav
log gets richer. The refinery shape again.

**Unratified — the dial needs a depth axis, not just frequency.** Killing a key
invalidates every executable doc that used it. Cascade radius is the difference
between a lesson and a wipe.

**Unratified — the dial belongs behind the curtain**, for the same reason as the
power budget and rarity: a self-set adversarialness knob gets turned down
exactly when it starts working.

**This is the biters, with a better job than punishing cheats.** Session 003's
formulation — *the only thing that can be permanently destroyed is the cheat
itself* — generalises: the only thing that can be permanently destroyed is the
thing that couldn't hold.

---

## 13. Fences and the defence surface

**Settled — after run 4, PreToolUse hooks on Bash, to stop the assistant
breaking things.** The point is not the protection; it's that building the
fences is the skill (improvement goal #6). A live adversary makes hook-writing
non-theoretical.

**Open — attack surface must match defence surface.** PreToolUse on Bash stops
destruction *through Bash*. If destruction is a state mutation inside the MCP
server, no Bash hook sees it — the call is `mcp__<server>__<tool>`, not a shell
command. PreToolUse does match MCP tool names, so it's coverable, but a
Bash-only fence points at empty ground. Worth resolving before run 4 rather
than after.

**Unratified — the hooks face the same selection pressure as the ontology.** A
fence that is too broad protects brittle keys along with sound ones, and the
schema calcifies behind it: safe and wrong. An over-blocking hook fails by
*preserving* something that should have died, which is far harder to notice than
a hook that lets something through. That makes it the failure mode to instrument
first.

**Prior art on hook reliability.** Session 003 recorded two open Claude Code
issues — Read bypassing PreToolUse hooks in the VSCode extension (#37540) and
`permissionDecision: deny` ignored for Edit (#37210). A fence you want to lean
on can be a hook; a fence that has to hold should be absence.

---

## 14. Open decisions, consolidated

Ordered by what blocks the most downstream work.

1. **Is an ontology entry prose-capped?** (§7) Whole design or whole leak.
2. **Derive the nav schema from existing entries.** (§4a) What axis every entry
   is written on. Blocks the research, not just a component.
3. **Scarcity curve before ore pairs.** (§6) The pairs fall out of it.
4. **Seed or per-tick dice.** (§11) Determines whether the world is learnable.
5. **Attack surface vs defence surface.** (§13) Resolve before run 4.
6. **Uranium: structure or word count, and when.** (§7)
7. **Brownout behaviour.** (§10) Refusal, proportional degradation, or priority.
8. **Cascade depth on the adversarialness dial.** (§12)
9. **Do action templates occupy crate slots?** (§8) Otherwise churn is free.
10. **Gate integrity verification.** (§3) Without it, time-to-trivial measures
    erosion, not learning.
11. **Does the nav schema itself evolve?** (§4a) Fixed = designed at minimum
    knowledge. Evolving = migration.
12. **Cartridge auditability scope.** (§1) Content hidden, mechanism public?

---

## 15. Superseded — the correction trail

Every claim below was proposed, then killed. Kept because the trail is the
demonstration: this is what the thinking actually looked like, including the
parts that were wrong. Ordered by when they died.

**1. The third surface is a private win condition, with a public tech tree.**
*Proposed:* the operator holds a hidden definition of "done" so the assistant
can't build a world that terminates conveniently; the improvement-goals list
becomes a visible tech tree that unlocks map to.
*Killed by:* the README says *this is work, not a video game* three times in a
row and twice more in the rules. A game-shaped answer to a research project.
*Stands now:* §1 — the third surface is the DoD, and it's not a peer surface but
the plane the other two are judged against.

**2. Directional control by up-front constraint.**
*Proposed:* every unlock must terminate at a declared node; the operator audits
the mapping without reading the content.
*Killed by:* drift isn't a failure to prevent, it's the yield. World-building is
an instance of the pipeline, and each failed world is data preventing the next.
*Stands now:* §11 — the world doesn't need to be aimed up front, it needs to be
*responsive*. Rarity as a continuous curtain lever does the aiming.
*Survived separately:* the trace/gate line itself (§1) — it was the
directional-control use that died, not the repo/server split.

**3. Science is write-only, therefore accumulation isn't learning.**
*Proposed:* nothing reads the Global Inbox back in, so capture without retrieval
is witnessing rather than preventing.
*Resolved by:* science is consumed at **gate-design time**, spent to build the
next constraint rather than read for reference.
*Stands now:* §3.

**4. MCP for the knowledge base because it survives resets.**
*Proposed by the operator; corrected.* Git already survives resets, permanently
and auditably, and a planet reset never touches `The Lab/`.
*Stands now:* §4 — the real reason is retrieval, and the persistence question is
answered instead by the Ship never resetting.

**5. Two servers with opposite lifecycles, as an open architecture question.**
*Killed by:* the Ship never resets. The permanent/disposable split has a
diegetic anchor and stops being a decision to make.
*Stands now:* §4.

**6. Cap the guy, not the miner.**
*Proposed:* one shared inventory of 5–10 slots regardless of how many patches
run, so parallelism buys variety rather than volume, fixing parallel-supply /
serial-consumption surplus.
*Killed by:* the bottleneck isn't inventory, it's that building a new miner to
deploy on a patch is expensive.
*Stands now:* §9.

**7. Sessions are the binding constraint.**
*Proposed:* radar can be uncapped because a found patch is inert until you cwd a
session into it — "you cannot mine more than you can sit in."
*Killed by:* 6–8 concurrent sessions is comfortable. Attention is not the
scarcity.
*Stands now:* §9 — discovery isn't capacity, and miner cost is what binds. The
conclusion survived; the reasoning was wrong.

**8. Rate-limit pressure as the power mechanic.**
*Proposed:* the rate limiter is structurally a power grid — shared pool,
concurrent draw, hard ceiling, fixed refill — so power doesn't need simulating.
*Killed by:* off by at least an order of magnitude. Doesn't bind until 10+ specs
run at once.
*Stands now:* §10a — artificial budget early behind the curtain, real grid
pressure arriving on its own at the DoD. Two sources, one handoff.

**9. The rarity lever is an operator cheat surface.**
*Proposed:* an operator-adjustable scarcity curve sets the whole design to
"adjustable"; fix by logging every change rather than blocking it.
*Killed by:* rarity was never the operator's lever. It's a curtain lever.
*Stands now:* §11 — the assistant holds it, and the logging requirement narrows
to *loosening only*.

**10. Deconstruct as an expensive, near-irreversible commitment.**
*Proposed:* if miners are the scarcest build, patch choice is the first hard
strategic call, made when you know least, and it needs an explicit refund rule.
*Killed by:* deconstruct and move-miner are ordinary skills costing one action
template. Cheap and reversible.
*Stands now:* §9 — and the real cost turns out to be the declaration, which is
what makes traceable routing the exhaust of playing.

**11. Brittleness as a metric computed over the corpus.**
*Proposed:* single-use, redundant, overloaded and vacuous keys are measurable
from the entries, so the destruction rule is auditable even though the world
isn't — the assistant executes the rule without authoring it.
*Killed by:* it shouldn't be a riddle to guess. Anything well built shouldn't be
brittle; anything brittle should break.
*Stands now:* §12 — destructibility **is** the test. No diagnosis step at all,
which removes the accountability problem the metric was invented to solve.

---

## 16. Carried forward, unresolved from session 003

**Per-directory skill locality is untested and this design rests on it.** The
guy-session mechanic requires skills to resolve per-directory. Live pickup
failed its only real test last session when the skill was in a parent directory;
the own-directory test has never been run. Recorded in
`The Lab/Global Inbox/2026-08-17_223326.md`.

**Obsidian Sync is a third writer.** It can dirty the git tree without passing
through the terminal, and the cheating detector cannot distinguish a pulled file
from a hand-edited one. Not yet in any spec.
```

### CLAUDE (claude-opus-5) · 2026-08-18 11:20:13 +0200

Read both in full. Here's where I've landed.

## What the two docs are

**World Design.md** is the structured surface — 16 sections, everything tagged Settled / Open / Unratified / Superseded, with a correction trail of 11 killed ideas. It's the assistant-side compilation.

**Creative constraints through bottlenecking key resources.md** is your raw design dump — the economy as you actually want it, including three whole subsystems World Design doesn't have.

## Where they contradict — and it's not trivial

**1. Prose is not actually capped.** World Design §7 opens with *"prose is capped absolutely and the cap never relaxes."* Your doc lists **water → CLAUDE.md prose** and **oil → ontology prose** as minable primaries, plus **pipe → water to human (liquid storage tank for drinking) for lexicon prose**. That's prose as a *metered, purchasable* resource, not a fixed cap. It also silently answers open decision #1 (*"is an ontology entry prose-capped?"* — flagged as "whole design or whole leak"): your answer is that ontology prose is bought with oil, so it's rationed by mine rate rather than word count. Those are different systems. One of them has to lose.

**2. Three subsystems exist only in your doc.**
- **Smelting** — 5:1 secondaries (steel = custom templates/blueprints, stone brick = defensive hooks, condensed fuel = 10x power). World Design has no refining tier at all.
- **Transport machines** with distinct semantics — inserter local, belt one-shot-anywhere, roboport unlimited-but-cooldown, pipe for water. World Design mentions inserters only as a power consumer.
- **The assembler ladder** — assembler 1 = tasks, 2 = builds, 3 = projects. That maps *exactly* onto the DoD ladder in §3 (task → spec → decomposed project). It's the cleanest fit in either document and World Design doesn't make the connection.

**3. Who consumes science.** World Design §3 resolved it: science is consumed by the *assistant*, at gate-design time. Your doc: *"Research Questions feed raw docs to ShipNav and then to a Lab... which exhausts a science — running a lab session, which costs lots of power."* That's the *operator* spending it. Different consumer, different economy.

**4. Two hook-purchase paths in your own doc.** Body armor (iron) alters PreToolUse hooks; stone brick is "defensive tool hooks." Both buy the same thing.

**5. Wood exists in your doc** (power transport) and nowhere in World Design.

Also worth flagging: your doc's power model is **estimated token spend per minute** — a rate. §10a settled on a *budget*. Rate is the tighter mechanic and it forces caching and model routing harder, which is what §10a says it wants.

## The dirty tree

It's one move, not a mess. Everything was reorganised outside git:

| From | To |
|---|---|
| `The Ship/Faktor-AI v2/World Design.md` | `The Ship/Fireproof Safe/Game Improvement/World Design.md` |
| `The Ship/Starting Items/*.png` | `The Ship/Fireproof Safe/Media/` |
| `Miners/Miner-001-Science/Science Ore Patch.md` | `The Ship/Fireproof Safe/Ancient Artifacts/` |
| `_THE TUTORIAL/*` | `The Ship/Hitchhiker's Guide/` |

I diffed the moved World Design — byte-identical, pure rename. Plus genuinely new: `REPO ROOT LEVEL BOOT.md`, `The Ship/SatNav/`, `The Ship/Distress Beacon/README.md`, a Captain's Log for today, two new Media files, and the two Game Improvement docs. Modified: `CLAUDE.md`, `README.md`, `.obsidian/daily-notes.json`.

34 untracked paths, 25 deletes, 3 modifies. `git add -A` will pair the deletes with the adds and record them as renames — nothing is lost, and it's reversible before commit.

**One question before I touch anything:** you said "fix the dirty tree, then trim it even more." Trimming *after* staging means the commit history shows an add-then-delete for anything you cut. Do you want to trim first so the tree lands clean in one commit, or stage the move as-is (so the reorg is a legible standalone commit) and trim in a second one? And when you say trim — files out of the repo, or the two design docs down against each other?

content="width=device-width,initial-scale=1"><style>:root{color-scheme:light}body{margin:0;padding:0;font:14px -apple-system,BlinkMacSystemFont,sans-serif;background:#faf9f5;color:#141413}img{max-width:100%}</style></head><body>
<title>Debris Protocol</title>
<style>
  :root {
    --bg:        #E7E8E6;
    --surface:   #F3F4F2;
    --surface-2: #DEE0DD;
    --ink:       #17191A;
    --ink-2:     #4A4F50;
    --ink-3:     #71787A;
    --rule:      #CDD0CD;
    --rust:      #A8481C;
    --rust-bg:   #F0E0D6;
    --verd:      #2C6360;
    --verd-bg:   #DAE6E4;
    --shadow:    rgba(20,25,26,.07);
  }
  @media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) {
    :root:not([data-theme="light"]) {
      --bg:        #131516;
      --surface:   #1B1E1F;
      --surface-2: #24282A;
      --ink:       #E6E8E7;
      --ink-2:     #A5ABAB;
      --ink-3:     #7C8384;
      --rule:      #2D3234;
      --rust:      #E07C46;
      --rust-bg:   #33211A;
      --verd:      #63ADA5;
      --verd-bg:   #172B2A;
      --shadow:    rgba(0,0,0,.35);
    }
  }
  :root[data-theme="dark"] {
    --bg:        #131516;
    --surface:   #1B1E1F;
    --surface-2: #24282A;
    --ink:       #E6E8E7;
    --ink-2:     #A5ABAB;
    --ink-3:     #7C8384;
    --rule:      #2D3234;
    --rust:      #E07C46;
    --rust-bg:   #33211A;
    --verd:      #63ADA5;
    --verd-bg:   #172B2A;
    --shadow:    rgba(0,0,0,.35);
  }

  * { box-sizing: border-box; }

  body {
    margin: 0;
    background: var(--bg);
    color: var(--ink);
    font-family: "Segoe UI", system-ui, -apple-system, "Helvetica Neue", sans-serif;
    font-size: 16.5px;
    line-height: 1.62;
    -webkit-text-size-adjust: 100%;
  }

  .wrap {
    max-width: 40rem;
    margin: 0 auto;
    padding: 2.5rem 1.25rem 5rem;
    display: flex;
    flex-direction: column;
    gap: 3rem;
  }

  .mono {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
  }

  /* ---- masthead ---- */

  header { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: .9rem; }

  .eyebrow {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .69rem;
    letter-spacing: .16em;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    color: var(--rust);
    display: flex;
    flex-wrap: wrap;
    gap: .55rem;
  }
  .eyebrow span::after { content: " /"; color: var(--ink-3); }
  .eyebrow span:last-child::after { content: ""; }

  h1 {
    margin: 0;
    font-size: 2.35rem;
    line-height: 1.06;
    letter-spacing: -.028em;
    font-weight: 700;
    text-wrap: balance;
  }

  .standfirst {
    margin: 0;
    font-size: 1.06rem;
    color: var(--ink-2);
    max-width: 34rem;
  }

  .meta {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .72rem;
    color: var(--ink-3);
    border-top: 1px solid var(--rule);
    padding-top: .8rem;
    display: flex;
    flex-wrap: wrap;
    gap: 1.1rem;
  }

  /* ---- sections ---- */

  section { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 1rem; }

  h2 {
    margin: 0;
    font-size: 1.32rem;
    letter-spacing: -.018em;
    font-weight: 700;
    padding-bottom: .5rem;
    border-bottom: 2px solid var(--ink);
    text-wrap: balance;
  }

  h3 {
    margin: 1rem 0 0;
    font-size: .95rem;
    font-weight: 700;
    letter-spacing: -.005em;
  }

  p { margin: 0; }
  p + p { margin-top: .85rem; }

  a { color: var(--rust); text-decoration-thickness: 1px; text-underline-offset: 2px; }
  a:focus-visible, summary:focus-visible {
    outline: 2px solid var(--rust);
    outline-offset: 3px;
    border-radius: 2px;
  }

  strong { font-weight: 700; }

  code {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .855em;
    background: var(--surface-2);
    padding: .1em .38em;
    border-radius: 3px;
  }

  ul { margin: 0; padding-left: 1.15rem; display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: .5rem; }
  li::marker { color: var(--ink-3); }

  /* ---- status chips ---- */

  .chip {
    display: inline-block;
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .63rem;
    letter-spacing: .1em;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    padding: .18em .5em;
    border-radius: 3px;
    vertical-align: .12em;
    white-space: nowrap;
  }
  .chip-settled { background: var(--verd-bg); color: var(--verd); }
  .chip-open    { background: var(--rust-bg); color: var(--rust); }

  /* ---- decision cards ---- */

  .decisions { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: .7rem; counter-reset: d; }

  .d {
    background: var(--surface);
    border: 1px solid var(--rule);
    border-left: 3px solid var(--verd);
    border-radius: 4px;
    padding: .95rem 1.05rem;
    display: flex;
    flex-direction: column;
    gap: .3rem;
    box-shadow: 0 1px 2px var(--shadow);
  }
  .d.blocking { border-left-color: var(--rust); }

  .d-head {
    display: flex;
    align-items: baseline;
    gap: .55rem;
    font-weight: 700;
    font-size: .97rem;
    line-height: 1.35;
  }
  .d-head::before {
    counter-increment: d;
    content: counter(d, decimal-leading-zero);
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .74rem;
    font-weight: 400;
    color: var(--ink-3);
    font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
    flex: none;
  }
  .d p { font-size: .93rem; color: var(--ink-2); padding-left: 1.85rem; }

  /* ---- fact rows ---- */

  .facts { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 0; }
  .fact {
    display: grid;
    grid-template-columns: 1fr;
    gap: .15rem;
    padding: .8rem 0;
    border-bottom: 1px solid var(--rule);
  }
  .fact:first-child { border-top: 1px solid var(--rule); }
  .fact-k {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .69rem;
    letter-spacing: .11em;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    color: var(--ink-3);
  }
  .fact-v { font-size: .94rem; }

  @media (min-width: 34rem) {
    .fact { grid-template-columns: 11rem 1fr; gap: 1rem; align-items: baseline; }
    .fact-k { padding-top: .12em; }
  }

  /* ---- callout ---- */

  .note {
    background: var(--surface);
    border: 1px solid var(--rule);
    border-radius: 4px;
    padding: 1rem 1.1rem;
    font-size: .93rem;
    color: var(--ink-2);
    display: flex;
    flex-direction: column;
    gap: .55rem;
  }
  .note .note-k {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .66rem;
    letter-spacing: .13em;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    color: var(--rust);
  }

  /* ---- flow ---- */

  .flow { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: 0; }
  .step {
    display: flex;
    gap: .85rem;
    padding: .7rem 0;
  }
  .step-n {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .7rem;
    color: var(--ink-3);
    font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
    flex: none;
    width: 1.6rem;
    padding-top: .28em;
  }
  .step-b { display: flex; flex-direction: column; gap: .18rem; }
  .step-t { font-weight: 700; font-size: .95rem; }
  .step-d { font-size: .9rem; color: var(--ink-2); }
  .step-surface {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .66rem;
    letter-spacing: .1em;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    color: var(--verd);
  }

  /* ---- table ---- */

  .scroll { overflow-x: auto; }
  table {
    border-collapse: collapse;
    width: 100%;
    min-width: 30rem;
    font-size: .88rem;
  }
  th, td {
    text-align: left;
    padding: .6rem .7rem .6rem 0;
    border-bottom: 1px solid var(--rule);
    vertical-align: top;
  }
  th {
    font-family: ui-monospace, "Cascadia Mono", "Consolas", "SF Mono", monospace;
    font-size: .66rem;
    letter-spacing: .11em;
    text-transform: uppercase;
    color: var(--ink-3);
    border-bottom: 1px solid var(--ink-3);
  }

  details {
    border-top: 1px solid var(--rule);
    padding-top: .8rem;
  }
  summary { cursor: pointer; font-weight: 700; font-size: .95rem; }
  details[open] summary { margin-bottom: .7rem; }

  footer {
    border-top: 1px solid var(--rule);
    padding-top: 1.2rem;
    font-size: .85rem;
    color: var(--ink-3);
  }
</style>

<div class="wrap">

  <header>
    <div class="eyebrow"><span>The Ship</span><span>V2</span><span>Session 003</span></div>
    <h1>Debris Protocol</h1>
    <p class="standfirst">A sandbox where prose is rationed, the file tree is evidence, and the only thing that can be permanently destroyed is cheating.</p>
    <div class="meta">
      <span>17 Aug 2026</span>
      <span>Review before build</span>
      <span>5 blocking decisions</span>
    </div>
  </header>

  <section>
    <h2>The principle</h2>
    <p><strong>Knowledge comes from friction. Friction comes from doing.</strong> Simple tasks get executed through the exact machinery that will later carry complex ones — the payload scales, the pipeline never changes.</p>
    <p>Two consequences that are easy to violate:</p>
    <ul>
      <li><strong>No toy path.</strong> No hardcoding the first few objects and building the real generator later. Friction against scaffolding you'll throw away teaches nothing, because the thing you fought isn't the thing that ships.</li>
      <li><strong>Failures surface loudly.</strong> Graceful degradation is failure-hiding with good manners. Every refusal names the precondition that failed and the state actually observed.</li>
    </ul>
    <div class="note">
      <div class="note-k">The cost, stated up front</div>
      <div>Session one produces a complete pipeline that emits one trivial object. Not something playable. That's the correct trade under this principle, but it's a slow first night and better seen now than resented at hour three.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2>What I need from you</h2>
    <p>Ordered by what blocks what. The first five stop the build; the rest I can default and you can override.</p>

    <div class="decisions">
      <div class="d blocking">
        <div class="d-head">Where is the line between the server and the repo?</div>
        <p>Hidden world content has to live behind the MCP server — that's the only place you can't read it. But the crate, the forms, cheat detection and provenance all have to be <em>files</em>, because they run on git. Everything else in this document sits on one side of that line. Nothing gets typed until it's drawn.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d blocking">
        <div class="d-head">What fields must a thing have?</div>
        <p>The schema the engine validates on load. If content doesn't conform, the server refuses to boot and says why. This is the gate that stops me adding anything untyped — it's your ontology rule expressed as data instead of as a hook, and it's what the generator needs first.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d blocking">
        <div class="d-head">How does a template declare its input slots?</div>
        <p>The cheat detector compares your diff against the declared slot list. Your <code>/science</code> template already does this informally — fixed questions, fenced answer blocks. Formalising that declaration is what makes the check deterministic instead of heuristic.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d blocking">
        <div class="d-head">What does processing emit?</div>
        <p>If it consumes the form and frees the slot, the loop is fill-and-clear — a chore. If the output is itself an item that occupies space downstream, you get real factory pressure and a reason for belts to exist. This is the difference between a rule and a base.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d blocking">
        <div class="d-head">Is the fence repo-wide or world-only?</div>
        <p>Repo-wide means you never hand-edit anything again — not the README, not engineer notes, not a typo. World-only is the reasonable choice. Repo-wide is the one that actually stops you typing in Obsidian. Changes every hook matcher either way.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d">
        <div class="d-head">Confirm Python</div>
        <p>You asked what most people use and I answered, but you never actually picked. Python dominates MCP adoption and got to the new spec first.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d">
        <div class="d-head">Does the prose budget grow?</div>
        <p>Debris gets four words. Either the budget doubles per tier so expressiveness is earned, or it never grows and you only ever get more <em>keys</em> — an endgame where you can describe the world precisely and never narrate it at all. The second is more brutal and probably more interesting.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d">
        <div class="d-head">Crate size, and is there a scrap verb?</div>
        <p>Starting slot count sets difficulty. And if processing is the only way to free a slot, a form you'll never fill is a dead slot forever. Factorio lets you trash items.</p>
      </div>
      <div class="d">
        <div class="d-head">The CLAUDE.md friction rule</div>
        <p>Proposed verbatim in the terminal, still unanswered. A behavioural constraint that depends on the constrained party remembering it isn't a constraint.</p>
      </div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2>Why V1 didn't work</h2>
    <p>V1 had machines and items and no actor. With no subject, every interaction defaults to prose <em>about</em> a system rather than an action <em>within</em> one — which is why 98% of it was typing in Obsidian. It also had no tick. A world with no actor and no tick cannot fail, and a system that cannot fail never asks you a question.</p>
    <p>V2 inverts the economics. In Obsidian, prose was free and structure was optional. Here <strong>structure is free and prose is the scarce resource</strong>, so the only way to say more is to invent a key. That's ontology design with the exit doors welded shut.</p>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2>The loop</h2>
    <p>Obsidian is not a canvas. It's an input slot.</p>
    <div class="flow">
      <div class="step">
        <div class="step-n">01</div>
        <div class="step-b">
          <div class="step-t">Generate</div>
          <div class="step-surface">Terminal</div>
          <div class="step-d">A blank form is stamped out, schema-conformant, occupying one crate slot. The verbatim prompt that caused it is attached in git at this moment.</div>
        </div>
      </div>
      <div class="step">
        <div class="step-n">02</div>
        <div class="step-b">
          <div class="step-t">Fill</div>
          <div class="step-surface">Obsidian</div>
          <div class="step-d">You type into declared input slots and nowhere else. Four words per value. This is the only legitimate thing you ever do in an editor.</div>
        </div>
      </div>
      <div class="step">
        <div class="step-n">03</div>
        <div class="step-b">
          <div class="step-t">Process</div>
          <div class="step-surface">Terminal</div>
          <div class="step-d">You run an action on the filled form, or a belt carries it to the next stage. The slot frees. What comes out the other side is decision 04.</div>
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
    <p>A filled form that never gets processed doesn't rot — it occupies a crate slot, so next time you have one fewer template available. Congestion, not decay: a backed-up belt stalls the factory, it doesn't eat your items.</p>
    <div class="note">
      <div class="note-k">Why this is the anti-Obsidian mechanic</div>
      <div>It prices generation. Making a new note cost nothing, so you made endlessly and nothing was forced to resolve. On a finite slot budget, unfinished work visibly crowds out new work — you can't start the interesting thing until you've closed the boring one. Structural, not motivational.</div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2>The fences</h2>

    <h3>Clean tree is the resting state</h3>
    <p>A <code>PostToolUse</code> hook commits after every write, so the tree returns to clean immediately. You never train yourself to remember to commit — the only writing path is one that commits. Dirty tree therefore stops meaning <em>sloppy</em> and starts meaning <em>cheating</em>.</p>
    <p>With one exception, and it's the interesting one: a generated form whose diff touches <strong>only its declared input slots</strong> is legal. That's an item sitting in a machine's input, mid-process. Anything else — new file, moved file, edited scaffold, a key that wasn't a slot — is a cheat.</p>
    <p>The check is cheap precisely <em>because</em> of auto-commit: the pristine generated version is already in git from its generation commit, so <code>git diff</code> shows exactly and only what you typed.</p>

    <h3>What the biters can eat</h3>
    <p>Everything legitimate is already committed, so damage can be maximally brutal with no mercy tier — it's all recoverable from history. The only thing that can be permanently destroyed is uncommitted work, which by definition is the cheat.</p>
    <p><strong>The punishment is exactly and only the destruction of the cheating.</strong> Nothing else can die.</p>

    <h3>Two layers, different jobs</h3>
    <div class="scroll">
      <table>
        <thead>
          <tr><th>Layer</th><th>Sees</th><th>Job</th></tr>
        </thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td><code>PreToolUse</code></td><td>My hands only</td><td>Fast feedback. Blocks in under a second with a reason. The teaching surface.</td></tr>
          <tr><td><code>pre-commit</code></td><td>Everything, including you at 1am</td><td>Actual enforcement. Catches what went around the sanctioned path.</td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>
    </div>
    <p>Neither covers everything. Together there's no seam — you can't write a forty-word value through me, and you can't write one behind my back without leaving evidence.</p>

    <h3>Two things this makes load-bearing</h3>
    <p><code>.gitignore</code> becomes the ontology of existence. Anything ignored is invisible to the detector, so ignored files are things that <em>don't exist in this world</em>. Obsidian churns <code>workspace.json</code> constantly and Windows drops <code>Thumbs.db</code>; every one is a false accusation unless explicitly declared nonexistent. That file stops being housekeeping.</p>
    <p>And the commit log becomes telemetry, not narration. Auto-commit-per-write is a high-fidelity action log with zero story. The narrative layer already exists elsewhere — transcripts, <code>/science</code>, engineer notes. Stop expecting commits to carry it.</p>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2>Architecture</h2>
    <p>An MCP server is the only place a world can hide from both of us. State lives behind a process, not in files — there's nothing to open, and <code>ls</code> tells you nothing. Tools are the guy's verbs, and a tool that refuses because a precondition failed is a terminal error in under a second.</p>

    <h3>You write the engine. I write the cartridge.</h3>
    <p>The server is content-blind: it loads world data it knows nothing about, validates it against your schema, and serves it. You write the machine, I write the ROM, you never read the ROM. You can't be surprised by a world you authored — handing me the content is the only way the fog works on you.</p>

    <h3>The July spec changed two things that land on this design</h3>
    <div class="facts">
      <div class="fact">
        <div class="fact-k">No more sessions</div>
        <div class="fact-v">The <code>initialize</code> handshake and <code>Mcp-Session-Id</code> are gone. Cross-call state uses explicit server-minted handles passed as ordinary tool arguments — so <strong>the guy's identity is a parameter on every call</strong>, never a global in your process. Build it as a global and it works on your machine and dies the moment it's hosted.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="fact">
        <div class="fact-k">Lists don't vary</div>
        <div class="fact-v"><code>tools/list</code> no longer varies per connection. Locally, single player, unlock-by-tool-appearing works fine. Hosted with other players you cannot serve A a different tool list than B — unlock becomes a refusal inside a tool that was always there.</div>
      </div>
    </div>
    <p style="margin-top:1rem"><strong>Absence is a single-player mechanic. Denial is the multiplayer one.</strong> Write the unlock rule once as <code>is_unlocked(guy, thing)</code> and let the surface decide how to express it — local stdio omits the tool, hosted HTTP refuses the call. Scatter <code>if despair &gt;= 5</code> through tool bodies and you'll rewrite the world to host it.</p>

    <h3>Despair</h3>
    <p>Don't tick what you can derive. Despair is <code>floor((now − start) / 30)</code>, computed on read. No daemon, deterministic, survives restarts — which a background loop doesn't. Same answer for accumulated damage: resolve what should have happened the next time anything looks.</p>
    <p>Which is thematically better anyway. You come back to the terminal and find out what happened while you were gone. That's exactly the experience of alt-tabbing back into Factorio.</p>
  </section>

  <section>
    <h2>Open on my side</h2>
    <p>Research and verification I owe you before anything gets built.</p>
    <ul>
      <li><strong>Can a hook see your verbatim prompt?</strong> <code>UserPromptSubmit</code> exists but the docs don't show the prompt text in its payload. The entire provenance mechanic depends on this. Unverified.</li>
      <li><strong>Does the fence actually hold on your surface?</strong> There are open issues where <code>PreToolUse</code> deny gets bypassed for Edit and Read. Before trusting it, write a deliberate violation and confirm it gets blocked — the most on-theme QA task this project could have.</li>
      <li><strong>SDK maturity.</strong> The spec shipped three weeks ago and it was breaking. Python SDK v2 is still labelled beta; FastMCP renamed its main class in v4. Fresh ground.</li>
    </ul>
  </section>

  <details>
    <summary>Reference: verified facts behind the above</summary>
    <div class="facts">
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">MCP spec</div><div class="fact-v">2026-07-28, final. Stateless core. Roots, Sampling and Logging deprecated. <code>toolsListChanged</code> survives, now opt-in via <code>subscriptions/listen</code>.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">Claude Code</div><div class="fact-v">2.1.233 on this machine. <code>list_changed</code> handled correctly since 2.1.0; since 2.1.214 a transient refresh error no longer wipes a server's tool list.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">Skill loading</div><div class="fact-v">Adding or editing a skill is picked up mid-session without restart — but a top-level skills directory that didn't exist at startup needs one. Nested <code>.claude/skills/</code> below the working dir load only after Claude reads or edits a file in that subdirectory: genuine fog of war, and free.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">SDK adoption</div><div class="fact-v">Python dominates by a wide margin. Python SDK v2 and FastMCP 4 both implement the new spec. TypeScript is equally Tier 1 but has no dominant high-level framework.</div></div>
    </div>
  </details>

  <section>
    <h2>Where we pick up</h2>
    <p><strong>Next session opens on one thing:</strong> designing the template you fill out for each new stage of the world created behind the MCP. Measured in <em>rollouts</em>, not hours.</p>
    <p>Hours measure effort, and this project is about friction — an hour of fighting a constraint and an hour of coasting look identical on a clock. A rollout is falsifiable: either a stage exists behind the server and can be played, or it doesn't.</p>

    <div class="note">
      <div class="note-k">Why that's decision 01 wearing a different hat</div>
      <div>The rollout template is the handoff form across the engine/cartridge line — what you have to specify before I can write a stage you've never seen. Designing it <em>is</em> deciding what crosses the boundary between server state and repo files. Next session resolves tonight's top blocker as a side effect rather than as homework.</div>
    </div>

    <p>It should also be the first real artifact through the pipeline: a generated form, declared slots, prose capped. If the rollout template can't survive its own constraints, better to find out before any world content depends on it.</p>

    <h3>Two things broke tonight, and both change the design</h3>
    <ul>
      <li><strong>Live skill pickup failed.</strong> A skill deployed to the repo-root <code>.claude/skills/</code> was not discoverable mid-session, despite the docs saying it would be and despite the one stated caveat not applying. Working hypothesis, untested: the watcher covers the <em>starting</em> directory's skills but not parents. The V2 unlock mechanic was built on live pickup, so it's now unconfirmed rather than settled. Test before building: write a skill into the session's own starting-directory skills folder and see if it resolves.</li>
      <li><strong>Obsidian Sync is a third writer.</strong> Bidirectional, merge strategy, no excluded folders. It pulls remote edits into the working tree — files no hook saw and no commit captured. A file arriving from your phone is indistinguishable from a hand-edit, so the cheat detector will accuse you of something you didn't do. It doesn't touch <code>.claude/</code>, but it does touch your notes. Not yet solved anywhere in this document.</li>
    </ul>

    <h3>Repo state at close</h3>
    <div class="facts">
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">Commits</div><div class="fact-v"><code>70e0e7b</code> the session, <code>5c159fe</code> the skill-pickup failure. Both pushed to <code>main</code>.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">CLAUDE.md</div><div class="fact-v">Friction rule added, approved verbatim — working around a fence you were meant to hit destroys the only thing the fence was for.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">Skills</div><div class="fact-v"><code>obsidian-sync</code> deployed at Factor-AI level, repointed to this vault, verified end to end. Needs a restart to be invokable.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">Science</div><div class="fact-v">Three entries in <code>The Lab/Global Inbox/</code>: prose-first surfaces no decisions, the no-toy-path principle, and the live-pickup failure.</div></div>
      <div class="fact"><div class="fact-k">Code written</div><div class="fact-v">None. No MCP server exists yet.</div></div>
    </div>
  </section>

  <footer>
    Session 003 · The Ship · brainstorm to spec, no code written.
    Everything above is reversible except the five decisions near the top, which everything else is waiting on.
  </footer>

</div>

</body></html>
```

### CLAUDE (claude-opus-5) · 2026-08-18 11:21:16 +0200

Read it. That's session 003's handoff doc — and it changes the picture, because World Design silently dropped several things it decided.

## What Debris Protocol settled that World Design never carries

**Auto-commit as the resting state.** A `PostToolUse` hook commits after every write, so clean tree is normal and **dirty tree stops meaning *sloppy* and starts meaning *cheating*.** The only legal dirty state is a generated form whose diff touches nothing but its declared input slots. World Design does not mention this once.

**Despair is derived, not ticked** — `floor((now − start) / 30)`, computed on read. No daemon, deterministic, survives restarts. World Design has no despair mechanic at all, and no derived-vs-ticked principle, even though §8's wall-clocked mine rate is the same problem.

**The July 2026 MCP spec constraints.** Stateless core, no `Mcp-Session-Id`, guy identity is a parameter on every call, and `tools/list` no longer varies per connection — so unlock-by-tool-appearing only works single-player. "Absence is a single-player mechanic. Denial is the multiplayer one." World Design §8 leans hard on sessions (*"miners only mine while a session is cwd'd to their directory"*) without ever touching this. Probably compatible — a Claude Code session isn't an MCP session — but nothing in World Design says so.

**Two dropped questions that were blocking.** DP #3 (*how does a template declare its input slots?*) is what makes cheat detection deterministic instead of heuristic — gone from World Design entirely. DP's scrap verb (a form you'll never fill is a dead slot forever) — also gone.

## Where the three docs disagree

| Question | Debris Protocol (003) | World Design | Your bottlenecking doc |
|---|---|---|---|
| Prose budget grows? | Open, leaned "never grows, only more keys" | **Settled: capped absolutely, never relaxes** | Water buys CLAUDE.md prose, oil buys ontology prose, pipe buys lexicon prose |
| Fence scope | Open: repo-wide or world-only | Not addressed — §13 asks a *different* question (Bash vs MCP) | Body armor (iron) alters hooks; stone brick = defensive hooks |
| Runtime | Open: "confirm Python" | Silent | Silent |

So the prose cap has now been settled and un-settled across three documents, and **the fence-scope question from 003 was never answered — it was replaced by a different fence question that got the same slot number.** Worth noticing: that's how a blocking decision disappears without anyone deciding it.

Also: DP's "what fields must a thing have?" (a *world-content* schema the server boots against) and World Design §4a's nav schema (a *knowledge-base entry* schema) are two different schemas. Both get called "the schema." That conflation will bite.

## The thing I have to say about the dirty tree

Debris Protocol's position is that a dirty tree is the cheat signal, and the tree is currently 34 untracked paths and 25 deletes — created by moving files around **outside the terminal**, in Obsidian or Explorer.

That is exactly the failure both prior docs flagged and neither solved. DP: *"A file arriving from your phone is indistinguishable from a hand-edit, so the cheat detector will accuse you of something you didn't do."* World Design §16 carries it forward as unsolved. It isn't hypothetical now — last night's reorg is precisely the untraceable third-writer event, and no hook saw any of it.

I'm not going to quietly `git add -A` and make that disappear. Under the friction rule that's routing around the fence. The reorg itself is fine and probably good — but it's the concrete evidence that **the auto-commit design from 003 was never built, and until it is, "dirty tree = cheating" cannot be turned on.**

## So, before I touch anything

Two real questions, and they're connected:

1. **Do we treat this reorg as a legitimate pre-hook move and commit it as one clean rename commit** — accepting that it's the last untraced bulk edit — **or does it need to be reconstructed through the terminal** so the trace exists? The first is pragmatic; the second is the doctrinal answer and costs you an hour.

2. **When you say "trimming it even more" — files, or documents?** If it's documents, the highest-value trim isn't cutting either design doc. It's reconciling all three into one, because right now the prose cap is settled in one, open in another, and priced as a resource in the third — and nobody can tell which is current.

### CLAUDE (claude-opus-5) · 2026-08-18 11:23:14 +0200

### CLAUDE (claude-opus-5) · 2026-08-18 11:23:34 +0200

Here's the situation: **22 commits, all on `main`, all from 17 Aug, zero tags, zero branches.** No reset has happened yet. You're designing this before the first one, which is the right time — retrofitting run boundaries after three resets would be archaeology.

## The thing to internalise first

Git never deletes anything. When you `rm -rf` the planet and commit, the old files are still in every commit that contained them, permanently. **A reset destroys the working tree, not the history.**

So your problem is not preservation — that's already solved and you get it free. Your problem is **navigation**: making it possible for someone to land on this repo and jump straight to "show me run 3" without reading 200 commit messages. You need named coordinates.

## The three tools, and why one of them is right

| Tool | What it is | Fit |
|---|---|---|
| **Branch** | A *mutable* pointer that moves as you commit. Meant for parallel lines of work. | Wrong. Your runs are sequential, not parallel. And a branch can be deleted by accident. |
| **Tag** | An *immutable* named coordinate on one commit. Annotated tags carry a message, author and date, and push to GitHub as browsable snapshots. | **Right.** This is what tags exist for. |
| **The reset commit itself** | A commit whose entire diff *is* the destruction. | Right, and complementary. Makes the boundary visible in the log rather than only in metadata. |

Use tags *and* a deliberate reset commit. Not branches.

## The ritual I'd propose

A reset becomes a fixed sequence, not an act of frustration at 1am. Four commits, two tags:

1. **The toll.** File the entry naming what the world failed at. Lands in The Ship. World Design §2 already requires this ("a reset is legal only after a filed entry") — this makes it mechanical rather than an honour system. Last commit of the run.
2. **Tag the end** on that commit.
3. **The scuttle.** One commit that deletes the planet and leaves The Ship. **Pure deletion — nothing else in it.** If you mix a deletion with additions the diff becomes unreadable, and this commit's whole job is to be readable.
4. **The landing.** Scaffold the new world.
5. **Tag the start** on the landing.

## What that buys — and this is the payoff

Once those coordinates exist, four commands answer everything:

```
git log  <start>..<end>          # the narrative of that run
git diff <start>  <end>  --stat  # what the run built
git show <scuttle>               # exactly what died, file by file
git diff <end> <next-start>      # what The Ship carried across
```

That last one is the good one. World Design §5 says *"if reset 8 still hurts as much as reset 2, the ontology isn't doing any work"* and calls it free to instrument. **It literally becomes a git command.** The diff across each reset boundary *is* the measurement — a scuttle diff that shrinks run over run is the ontology doing its job, and one that stays the same size is your falsification. You don't have to build an instrument; you have to plant the tags.

Two more things it gets you:

- **GitHub renders tags as browsable snapshots.** A viewer clicks a tag and sees the whole repo as it stood at that moment — not a diff, the actual world. That's your building-in-public artifact and it's the natural cut point for a 4–6 minute segment.
- **"The Ship never resets" becomes enforceable rather than trusted.** A scuttle commit is mechanically checkable: assert it deletes nothing under `The Ship/`. That's a real fence, and it's the kind you were going to have to write anyway.

## What I need from you

**The naming convention is yours to set, not mine to guess.** Your improvement goals name exactly this — no pattern-matching on naming conventions. Three shapes so you have something to react to:

- `run-001-start` / `run-001-end` — flat, sorts correctly, boring, works
- `run-001/landing` / `run-001/scuttle` — GitHub groups these in a folder in the tag list
- Something diegetic — the Ship's log already has voice, and these tags are the ledger's spine

Pick one, and tell me whether a run and a session are the same unit or different. Right now the log has explicit session markers (`f007148` "Session 002 close", `70e0e7b` "Session 003") but no run markers, and if sessions and runs are different units you need two coordinate systems, not one.

Then the sequence is: commit last night's reorg → tag the current tip as the origin → and from that point the ledger is legible forward.
